My supervisor made a whole thing of it, though, and then the occupational health nurse got involved, so… that’s why I’m here, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying it like that, because it sounds dramatic. But if the scale goes up even a little, I get this panic, like everything I’ve worked to keep under control is going to just unravel and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nIt’s stupid because it’s just a number, right? But it doesn’t feel like just a number. It feels like… like when a machine starts making the wrong noise and you know something’s about to go off, even if everyone else says it’s fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight?\n\nPatient: Not really… I know people say I do, but when I catch myself in the shop window, or in the bathroom mirror, I don’t see that. I just see my stomach and my thighs, like they’re too big, and then I can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nIt sounds vain, I guess. Like, I’ll be trying to get back to work and I’m still stuck on how I looked for the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… what counts as “large”? Like, people at work will call a sandwich and chips a big lunch, but those guys are standing around the break room eating like it’s nothing, talking about football pools and whose turn it is to clean the press filters.\n\nFor me, I don’t really think of it that way. I can’t tell if something is a large amount or if it just feels large because I’m paying too much attention to it. I guess I’d need to know what you mean by “large,” because my sense of it is kind of messed up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean, “lost control” sounds like… like I’m suddenly eating everything in the cupboards, and I don’t think of it like that.

Sometimes I feel kind of foggy around food, like I’m not doing it “right,” but then I get stuck thinking about the exact thing I ate, or whether I should’ve had it earlier in the day. At work, if I eat on break, the machines are so loud and everyone’s coming in and out, and I’ll be trying to act normal, but my head is somewhere else.

So… I don’t know if that’s losing control, exactly. It’s more like I’m tense the whole time.

I don’t think so… but I guess I don’t always know what counts as “excessive.” Like, if someone else saw it, they might have a totally different opinion, you know?

But then I’ll think about what I had and it feels like too much even if it probably wasn’t.

But then I’ll think about what I had and it feels like too much even if it probably wasn’t.\n\nSo… in a short period of time? I haven’t noticed anything like that, not really. I don’t know if there’s a number, exactly. People always talk about numbers like that makes it simple, but it doesn’t feel simple to me.

It’s more like… when I catch myself in the shop window at work, or in the bathroom mirror, I just zero in on my stomach and my thighs. Like that’s all I can see. They look too big to me, and then it just… it can wreck my whole mood for the rest of the day.

And I know the shop windows are warped sometimes—one of the guys was joking about that once because the reflection makes the forklifts look bent—but even knowing that doesn’t really help. I still see it and feel awful.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… occupational health sent me over. They said my weight is, like, dangerously low for what I do at work—running presses and fabrication all day. I work around metal and plastics, and it gets hot, and everyone’s always acting like I’m about to fall over, but I don’t really see it that way.\n\nI just thought I was getting leaner, you know? More disciplined. Like when you get your routine right and things start tightening up. My supervisor made a whole thing of it, though, and then the occupational health nurse got involved, so… that’s why I’m here, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying it like that, because it sounds dramatic. But if the scale goes up even a little, I get this panic, like everything I’ve worked to keep under control is going to just unravel and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nIt’s stupid because it’s just a number, right? But it doesn’t feel like just a number. It feels like… like when a machine starts making the wrong noise and you know something’s about to go off, even if everyone else says it’s fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight?\n\nPatient: Not really… I know people say I do, but when I catch myself in the shop window, or in the bathroom mirror, I don’t see that. I just see my stomach and my thighs, like they’re too big, and then I can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nIt sounds vain, I guess. Like, I’ll be trying to get back to work and I’m still stuck on how I looked for the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… what counts as “large”? Like, people at work will call a sandwich and chips a big lunch, but those guys are standing around the break room eating like it’s nothing, talking about football pools and whose turn it is to clean the press filters.\n\nFor me, I don’t really think of it that way. I can’t tell if something is a large amount or if it just feels large because I’m paying too much attention to it. I guess I’d need to know what you mean by “large,” because my sense of it is kind of messed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “lost control” sounds like… like I’m suddenly eating everything in the cupboards, and I don’t think of it like that.\n\nSometimes I feel kind of foggy around food, like I’m not doing it “right,” but then I get stuck thinking about the exact thing I ate, or whether I should’ve had it earlier in the day. At work, if I eat on break, the machines are so loud and everyone’s coming in and out, and I’ll be trying to act normal, but my head is somewhere else.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that’s losing control, exactly. and it can ruin how I feel about myself for the rest of the day.\n- key: S084\n  name: Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition\n  description: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.\n  manifestation: My family keeps saying I look sick and the shop nurse acted like it was an emergency, but I keep telling them I’m still working double shifts, running, and getting things done, so it can’t be that serious.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: Since occupational health flagged my weight, I’ve been kept off some press work and pressured to get urgent care, which has disrupted my shifts and made it harder to keep up with bills at home.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 45 / metal_worker_or_plastic_worker / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S044\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help with weight loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure. I know what laxatives are, I think, but diuretics—are those the water pills? My aunt used to talk about those because her ankles would swell up in the summer, and she’d sit with her feet up and complain about the humidity.\n\nI mean, I don’t have some routine with that kind of thing, if that’s what you’re asking. I get confused because people talk about “bloating” all the time, especially at work—someone’s always saying they feel puffy after lunch or whatever—and then I start wondering if I’m supposed to be doing something about it, but… no, I don’t think I can give you a clear yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… but I guess I don’t always know what counts as “excessive.” Like, if someone else saw it, they might have a totally different opinion, you know?\n\nAt work, people eat so fast on break because the buzzer goes and then everyone’s back to the line, and someone will have a sandwich and chips and one of those giant gas station drinks, and it just seems normal there. But then I’ll think about what I had and it feels like too much even if it probably wasn’t.\n\nSo… in a short period of time? I haven’t noticed anything like that, not really. I’d probably need you to tell me what you mean by excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific weight or body shape you're trying to achieve?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s a number, exactly. Like that’s all I can see. They look too big to me, and then it just… it can wreck my whole mood for the rest of the day.\n\nAnd I know the shop windows are warped sometimes—one of the guys was joking about that once because the reflection makes the forklifts look bent—but even knowing that doesn’t really help. I don’t really know. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m eating fast,” if that makes sense. Sometimes at work everybody eats quickly because the breaks are so short, and you kind of learn to chew and swallow and get back before someone starts yelling about the line.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… occupational health sent me over. They said my weight is, like, dangerously low for what I do at work—running presses and fabrication all day. I work around metal and plastics, and it gets hot, and everyone’s always acting like I’m about to fall over, but I don’t really see it that way.\n\nI just thought I was getting leaner, you know? More disciplined. Like when you get your routine right and things start tightening up. My supervisor made a whole thing of it, though, and then the occupational health nurse got involved, so… that’s why I’m here, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying it like that, because it sounds dramatic. But if the scale goes up even a little, I get this panic, like everything I’ve worked to keep under control is going to just unravel and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nIt’s stupid because it’s just a number, right? But it doesn’t feel like just a number. It feels like… like when a machine starts making the wrong noise and you know something’s about to go off, even if everyone else says it’s fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight?\n\nPatient: Not really… I know people say I do, but when I catch myself in the shop window, or in the bathroom mirror, I don’t see that. I just see my stomach and my thighs, like they’re too big, and then I can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nIt sounds vain, I guess. Like, I’ll be trying to get back to work and I’m still stuck on how I looked for the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… what counts as “large”? Like, people at work will call a sandwich and chips a big lunch, but those guys are standing around the break room eating like it’s nothing, talking about football pools and whose turn it is to clean the press filters.\n\nFor me, I don’t really think of it that way. I can’t tell if something is a large amount or if it just feels large because I’m paying too much attention to it. I guess I’d need to know what you mean by “large,” because my sense of it is kind of messed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “lost control” sounds like… like I’m suddenly eating everything in the cupboards, and I don’t think of it like that.\n\nSometimes I feel kind of foggy around food, like I’m not doing it “right,” but then I get stuck thinking about the exact thing I ate, or whether I should’ve had it earlier in the day. At work, if I eat on break, the machines are so loud and everyone’s coming in and out, and I’ll be trying to act normal, but my head is somewhere else.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that’s losing control, exactly. It’s more like I’m tense the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I don’t like feeling food in my stomach, if that makes sense, but making myself vomit sounds very specific.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it that way. Sometimes after eating I’ll go to the bathroom just to be away from everyone, especially at work because the break room is horrible and there’s always someone banging a locker or asking if I’ve seen some tool they lost. But… I don’t know. I’m not sure what counts as “making yourself,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help with weight loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure. I know what laxatives are, I think, but diuretics—are those the water pills? I get confused because people talk about “bloating” all the time, especially at work—someone’s always saying they feel puffy after lunch or whatever—and then I start wondering if I’m supposed to be doing something about it, but… no, I don’t think I can give you a clear yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… but I guess I don’t always know what counts as “excessive.” Like, if someone else saw it, they might have a totally different opinion, you know?\n\nAt work, people eat so fast on break because the buzzer goes and then everyone’s back to the line, and someone will have a sandwich and chips and one of those giant gas station drinks, and it just seems normal there. But then I’ll think about what I had and it feels like too much even if it probably wasn’t.\n\nSo… in a short period of time? I haven’t noticed anything like that, not really. I’d probably need you to tell me what you mean by excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific weight or body shape you're trying to achieve?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s a number, exactly. They look too big to me, and then it just… it can wreck my whole mood for the rest of the day.\n\nAnd I know the shop windows are warped sometimes—one of the guys was joking about that once because the reflection makes the forklifts look bent—but even knowing that doesn’t really help. I still see it and feel awful.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you're focused on food?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m eating fast,” if that makes sense. Sometimes at work everybody eats quickly because the breaks are so short, and you kind of learn to chew and swallow and get back before someone starts yelling about the line.\n\nBut during times I’m focused on food… I’m not sure I’d call it “much more quickly than usual.” I notice more what it is, or how it feels after, not so much the speed. I might need you to explain what you mean by “during these times.”\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like you're eating an amount of food that would be considered unusual or abnormal by others?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean, “unusual” is hard because everyone I’m around eats different, you know? Like at the plant, some people barely eat on break and some people bring this huge lunch bag like they’re feeding three people, and nobody really says anything unless someone’s food smells strong in the break room.

For me… I don’t think I’ve had something where other people would be like, “That’s abnormal.” But then I also don’t really like eating where people can see, so maybe I wouldn’t know. Sometimes I’ll look at what’s on my plate and it feels like a lot to me, but I don’t know if someone else would think that or if it’s just me making it into a thing.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… occupational health sent me over. They said my weight is, like, dangerously low for what I do at work—running presses and fabrication all day. I work around metal and plastics, and it gets hot, and everyone’s always acting like I’m about to fall over, but I don’t really see it that way.\n\nI just thought I was getting leaner, you know? More disciplined. Like when you get your routine right and things start tightening up. My supervisor made a whole thing of it, though, and then the occupational health nurse got involved, so… that’s why I’m here, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying it like that, because it sounds dramatic. But if the scale goes up even a little, I get this panic, like everything I’ve worked to keep under control is going to just unravel and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nIt’s stupid because it’s just a number, right? But it doesn’t feel like just a number. It feels like… like when a machine starts making the wrong noise and you know something’s about to go off, even if everyone else says it’s fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight?\n\nPatient: Not really… I know people say I do, but when I catch myself in the shop window, or in the bathroom mirror, I don’t see that. I just see my stomach and my thighs, like they’re too big, and then I can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nIt sounds vain, I guess. Like, I’ll be trying to get back to work and I’m still stuck on how I looked for the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… what counts as “large”? Like, people at work will call a sandwich and chips a big lunch, but those guys are standing around the break room eating like it’s nothing, talking about football pools and whose turn it is to clean the press filters.\n\nFor me, I don’t really think of it that way. I can’t tell if something is a large amount or if it just feels large because I’m paying too much attention to it. I guess I’d need to know what you mean by “large,” because my sense of it is kind of messed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “lost control” sounds like… like I’m suddenly eating everything in the cupboards, and I don’t think of it like that.\n\nSometimes I feel kind of foggy around food, like I’m not doing it “right,” but then I get stuck thinking about the exact thing I ate, or whether I should’ve had it earlier in the day. At work, if I eat on break, the machines are so loud and everyone’s coming in and out, and I’ll be trying to act normal, but my head is somewhere else.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that’s losing control, exactly. It’s more like I’m tense the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I don’t like feeling food in my stomach, if that makes sense, but making myself vomit sounds very specific.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it that way. Sometimes after eating I’ll go to the bathroom just to be away from everyone, especially at work because the break room is horrible and there’s always someone banging a locker or asking if I’ve seen some tool they lost. But… I don’t know. I’m not sure what counts as “making yourself,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help with weight loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure. I know what laxatives are, I think, but diuretics—are those the water pills? My aunt used to talk about those because her ankles would swell up in the summer, and she’d sit with her feet up and complain about the humidity.\n\nI mean, I don’t have some routine with that kind of thing, if that’s what you’re asking. I get confused because people talk about “bloating” all the time, especially at work—someone’s always saying they feel puffy after lunch or whatever—and then I start wondering if I’m supposed to be doing something about it, but… no, I don’t think I can give you a clear yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… but I guess I don’t always know what counts as “excessive.” Like, if someone else saw it, they might have a totally different opinion, you know?\n\nAt work, people eat so fast on break because the buzzer goes and then everyone’s back to the line, and someone will have a sandwich and chips and one of those giant gas station drinks, and it just seems normal there. But then I’ll think about what I had and it feels like too much even if it probably wasn’t.\n\nSo… in a short period of time? I haven’t noticed anything like that, not really. I’d probably need you to tell me what you mean by excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific weight or body shape you're trying to achieve?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s a number, exactly. People always talk about numbers like that makes it simple, but it doesn’t feel simple to me.\n\nIt’s more like… when I catch myself in the shop window at work, or in the bathroom mirror, I just zero in on my stomach and my thighs. Like that’s all I can see. They look too big to me, and then it just… it can wreck my whole mood for the rest of the day.\n\nAnd I know the shop windows are warped sometimes—one of the guys was joking about that once because the reflection makes the forklifts look bent—but even knowing that doesn’t really help. I still see it and feel awful.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you're focused on food?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m eating fast,” if that makes sense. Sometimes at work everybody eats quickly because the breaks are so short, and you kind of learn to chew and swallow and get back before someone starts yelling about the line.\n\nBut during times I’m focused on food… I’m not sure I’d call it “much more quickly than usual.” I notice more what it is, or how it feels after, not so much the speed. I might need you to explain what you mean by “during these times.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating an amount of food that would be considered unusual or abnormal by others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “unusual” is hard because everyone I’m around eats different, you know? Like at the plant, some people barely eat on break and some people bring this huge lunch bag like they’re feeding three people, and nobody really says anything unless someone’s food smells strong in the break room.\n\nFor me… I don’t think I’ve had something where other people would be like, “That’s abnormal.” But then I also don’t really like eating where people can see, so maybe I wouldn’t know. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Like… “can’t stop” sounds really dramatic, like someone in a movie eating straight from the fridge at midnight or something.

I mean, once I start eating, sometimes I get kind of irritated because then I’m thinking about it more, and I’m aware of every bite, and it’s hard to just be normal about it. But I don’t know that it’s like I physically can’t stop.

At work it’s different too, because you’ve only got so much time, and someone’s always banging a pallet jack into something or the microwave is beeping forever, and half the time I’m more focused on getting back than on whether I’m full or not. So… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it as “I can’t stop once I start.”

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… occupational health sent me over. They said my weight is, like, dangerously low for what I do at work—running presses and fabrication all day. I work around metal and plastics, and it gets hot, and everyone’s always acting like I’m about to fall over, but I don’t really see it that way.\n\nI just thought I was getting leaner, you know? More disciplined. Like when you get your routine right and things start tightening up. My supervisor made a whole thing of it, though, and then the occupational health nurse got involved, so… that’s why I’m here, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying it like that, because it sounds dramatic. But if the scale goes up even a little, I get this panic, like everything I’ve worked to keep under control is going to just unravel and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nIt’s stupid because it’s just a number, right? But it doesn’t feel like just a number. It feels like… like when a machine starts making the wrong noise and you know something’s about to go off, even if everyone else says it’s fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight?\n\nPatient: Not really… I know people say I do, but when I catch myself in the shop window, or in the bathroom mirror, I don’t see that. I just see my stomach and my thighs, like they’re too big, and then I can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nIt sounds vain, I guess. Like, I’ll be trying to get back to work and I’m still stuck on how I looked for the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… what counts as “large”? Like, people at work will call a sandwich and chips a big lunch, but those guys are standing around the break room eating like it’s nothing, talking about football pools and whose turn it is to clean the press filters.\n\nFor me, I don’t really think of it that way. I can’t tell if something is a large amount or if it just feels large because I’m paying too much attention to it. I guess I’d need to know what you mean by “large,” because my sense of it is kind of messed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “lost control” sounds like… like I’m suddenly eating everything in the cupboards, and I don’t think of it like that.\n\nSometimes I feel kind of foggy around food, like I’m not doing it “right,” but then I get stuck thinking about the exact thing I ate, or whether I should’ve had it earlier in the day. At work, if I eat on break, the machines are so loud and everyone’s coming in and out, and I’ll be trying to act normal, but my head is somewhere else.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that’s losing control, exactly. It’s more like I’m tense the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I don’t like feeling food in my stomach, if that makes sense, but making myself vomit sounds very specific.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it that way. Sometimes after eating I’ll go to the bathroom just to be away from everyone, especially at work because the break room is horrible and there’s always someone banging a locker or asking if I’ve seen some tool they lost. But… I don’t know. I’m not sure what counts as “making yourself,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help with weight loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure. I know what laxatives are, I think, but diuretics—are those the water pills? My aunt used to talk about those because her ankles would swell up in the summer, and she’d sit with her feet up and complain about the humidity.\n\nI mean, I don’t have some routine with that kind of thing, if that’s what you’re asking. I get confused because people talk about “bloating” all the time, especially at work—someone’s always saying they feel puffy after lunch or whatever—and then I start wondering if I’m supposed to be doing something about it, but… no, I don’t think I can give you a clear yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… but I guess I don’t always know what counts as “excessive.” Like, if someone else saw it, they might have a totally different opinion, you know?\n\nAt work, people eat so fast on break because the buzzer goes and then everyone’s back to the line, and someone will have a sandwich and chips and one of those giant gas station drinks, and it just seems normal there. But then I’ll think about what I had and it feels like too much even if it probably wasn’t.\n\nSo… in a short period of time? I haven’t noticed anything like that, not really. I’d probably need you to tell me what you mean by excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific weight or body shape you're trying to achieve?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s a number, exactly. People always talk about numbers like that makes it simple, but it doesn’t feel simple to me.\n\nIt’s more like… when I catch myself in the shop window at work, or in the bathroom mirror, I just zero in on my stomach and my thighs. Like that’s all I can see. They look too big to me, and then it just… it can wreck my whole mood for the rest of the day.\n\nAnd I know the shop windows are warped sometimes—one of the guys was joking about that once because the reflection makes the forklifts look bent—but even knowing that doesn’t really help. I still see it and feel awful.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you're focused on food?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m eating fast,” if that makes sense. Sometimes at work everybody eats quickly because the breaks are so short, and you kind of learn to chew and swallow and get back before someone starts yelling about the line.\n\nBut during times I’m focused on food… I’m not sure I’d call it “much more quickly than usual.” I notice more what it is, or how it feels after, not so much the speed. I might need you to explain what you mean by “during these times.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating an amount of food that would be considered unusual or abnormal by others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “unusual” is hard because everyone I’m around eats different, you know? Like at the plant, some people barely eat on break and some people bring this huge lunch bag like they’re feeding three people, and nobody really says anything unless someone’s food smells strong in the break room.\n\nFor me… I don’t think I’ve had something where other people would be like, “That’s abnormal.” But then I also don’t really like eating where people can see, so maybe I wouldn’t know. Sometimes I’ll look at what’s on my plate and it feels like a lot to me, but I don’t know if someone else would think that or if it’s just me making it into a thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating without being able to stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Like… “can’t stop” sounds really dramatic, like someone in a movie eating straight from the fridge at midnight or something.\n\nI mean, once I start eating, sometimes I get kind of irritated because then I’m thinking about it more, and I’m aware of every bite, and it’s hard to just be normal about it. But I don’t know that it’s like I physically can’t stop.\n\nAt work it’s different too, because you’ve only got so much time, and someone’s always banging a pallet jack into something or the microwave is beeping forever, and half the time I’m more focused on getting back than on whether I’m full or not. So… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it as “I can’t stop once I start.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like at the plant, some people barely eat on break and some people bring this huge lunch bag like they’re feeding three people, and nobody really says anything unless someone’s food smells strong in the break room.\n\nFor me… I don’t think I’ve had something where other people would be like, “That’s abnormal.” But then I also don’t really like eating where people can see, so maybe I wouldn’t know. Sometimes I’ll look at what’s on my plate and it feels like a lot to me, but I don’t know if someone else would think that or if it’s just me making it into a thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating without being able to stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Like… “can’t stop” sounds really dramatic, like someone in a movie eating straight from the fridge at midnight or something.\n\nI mean, once I start eating, sometimes I get kind of irritated because then I’m thinking about it more, and I’m aware of every bite, and it’s hard to just be normal about it. But I don’t know that it’s like I physically can’t stop.\n\nAt work it’s different too, because you’ve only got so much time, and someone’s always banging a pallet jack into something or the microwave is beeping forever, and half the time I’m more focused on getting back than on whether I’m full or not. So… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it as “I can’t stop once I start.”\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like you're eating a normal amount of food, but then you continue eating even after you feel full?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 13\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I mean, “full” is kind of a weird word for me, because sometimes you eat and you’re supposed to feel something, but mostly I’m just thinking about whether it was too much or whether I should’ve had something different.

Like at work, if I bring something, I’m usually watching the clock more than anything. The break room is so loud—people scraping chairs, somebody talking about overtime, someone complaining about the vending machine taking their dollar—and I’ll just kind of get through whatever I decided to eat. I don’t know if I’d say I keep going after I’m full.

I guess I haven’t really noticed it that way. If anything, I’m usually trying to figure out if I should be eating at all, not thinking, “Oh, I’m full but I’m still going.” But maybe I’m not answering it right.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… occupational health sent me over. They said my weight is, like, dangerously low for what I do at work—running presses and fabrication all day. I work around metal and plastics, and it gets hot, and everyone’s always acting like I’m about to fall over, but I don’t really see it that way.\n\nI just thought I was getting leaner, you know? More disciplined. Like when you get your routine right and things start tightening up. My supervisor made a whole thing of it, though, and then the occupational health nurse got involved, so… that’s why I’m here, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying it like that, because it sounds dramatic. But if the scale goes up even a little, I get this panic, like everything I’ve worked to keep under control is going to just unravel and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nIt’s stupid because it’s just a number, right? But it doesn’t feel like just a number. It feels like… like when a machine starts making the wrong noise and you know something’s about to go off, even if everyone else says it’s fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight?\n\nPatient: Not really… I know people say I do, but when I catch myself in the shop window, or in the bathroom mirror, I don’t see that. I just see my stomach and my thighs, like they’re too big, and then I can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nIt sounds vain, I guess. Like, I’ll be trying to get back to work and I’m still stuck on how I looked for the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… what counts as “large”? Like, people at work will call a sandwich and chips a big lunch, but those guys are standing around the break room eating like it’s nothing, talking about football pools and whose turn it is to clean the press filters.\n\nFor me, I don’t really think of it that way. I can’t tell if something is a large amount or if it just feels large because I’m paying too much attention to it. I guess I’d need to know what you mean by “large,” because my sense of it is kind of messed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “lost control” sounds like… like I’m suddenly eating everything in the cupboards, and I don’t think of it like that.\n\nSometimes I feel kind of foggy around food, like I’m not doing it “right,” but then I get stuck thinking about the exact thing I ate, or whether I should’ve had it earlier in the day. At work, if I eat on break, the machines are so loud and everyone’s coming in and out, and I’ll be trying to act normal, but my head is somewhere else.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that’s losing control, exactly. It’s more like I’m tense the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I don’t like feeling food in my stomach, if that makes sense, but making myself vomit sounds very specific.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it that way. Sometimes after eating I’ll go to the bathroom just to be away from everyone, especially at work because the break room is horrible and there’s always someone banging a locker or asking if I’ve seen some tool they lost. But… I don’t know. I’m not sure what counts as “making yourself,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help with weight loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure. I know what laxatives are, I think, but diuretics—are those the water pills? My aunt used to talk about those because her ankles would swell up in the summer, and she’d sit with her feet up and complain about the humidity.\n\nI mean, I don’t have some routine with that kind of thing, if that’s what you’re asking. I get confused because people talk about “bloating” all the time, especially at work—someone’s always saying they feel puffy after lunch or whatever—and then I start wondering if I’m supposed to be doing something about it, but… no, I don’t think I can give you a clear yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… but I guess I don’t always know what counts as “excessive.” Like, if someone else saw it, they might have a totally different opinion, you know?\n\nAt work, people eat so fast on break because the buzzer goes and then everyone’s back to the line, and someone will have a sandwich and chips and one of those giant gas station drinks, and it just seems normal there. But then I’ll think about what I had and it feels like too much even if it probably wasn’t.\n\nSo… in a short period of time? I haven’t noticed anything like that, not really. I’d probably need you to tell me what you mean by excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific weight or body shape you're trying to achieve?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s a number, exactly. People always talk about numbers like that makes it simple, but it doesn’t feel simple to me.\n\nIt’s more like… when I catch myself in the shop window at work, or in the bathroom mirror, I just zero in on my stomach and my thighs. Like that’s all I can see. They look too big to me, and then it just… it can wreck my whole mood for the rest of the day.\n\nAnd I know the shop windows are warped sometimes—one of the guys was joking about that once because the reflection makes the forklifts look bent—but even knowing that doesn’t really help. I still see it and feel awful.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you're focused on food?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m eating fast,” if that makes sense. Sometimes at work everybody eats quickly because the breaks are so short, and you kind of learn to chew and swallow and get back before someone starts yelling about the line.\n\nBut during times I’m focused on food… I’m not sure I’d call it “much more quickly than usual.” I notice more what it is, or how it feels after, not so much the speed. I mean, “unusual” is hard because everyone I’m around eats different, you know? Like at the plant, some people barely eat on break and some people bring this huge lunch bag like they’re feeding three people, and nobody really says anything unless someone’s food smells strong in the break room.\n\nFor me… I don’t think I’ve had something where other people would be like, “That’s abnormal.” But then I also don’t really like eating where people can see, so maybe I wouldn’t know. Sometimes I’ll look at what’s on my plate and it feels like a lot to me, but I don’t know if someone else would think that or if it’s just me making it into a thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating without being able to stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S043\n  name: Restrictive_Intake\n  description: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.\n  manifestation: Most days I keep it to coffee before the early shift, maybe half a protein bar at lunch, and a small bowl of vegetables after yoga, because eating more makes the whole day feel out of control.\n- key: S044\n  name: Low_Weight\n  description: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.\n  manifestation: They sent me from occupational health because they say my weight is dangerously low for someone running presses and doing fabrication all day, but I honestly thought I was just getting leaner and more disciplined.\n- key: S045\n  name: Fear_of_Weight_Gain\n  description: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.\n  manifestation: getting things done, so it can’t be that serious.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: Since occupational health flagged my weight, I’ve been kept off some metal_worker_or_plastic_worker / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S044\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating an amount of food that would be considered unusual or abnormal by others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “unusual” is hard because everyone I’m around eats different, you know? Like at the plant, some people barely eat on break and some people bring this huge lunch bag like they’re feeding three people, and nobody really says anything unless someone’s food smells strong in the break room.\n\nFor me… I don’t think I’ve had something where other people would be like, “That’s abnormal.” But then I also don’t really like eating where people can see, so maybe I wouldn’t know. Sometimes I’ll look at what’s on my plate and it feels like a lot to me, but I don’t know if someone else would think that or if it’s just me making it into a thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating without being able to stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Like… “can’t stop” sounds really dramatic, like someone in a movie eating straight from the fridge at midnight or something.\n\nI mean, once I start eating, sometimes I get kind of irritated because then I’m thinking about it more, and I’m aware of every bite, and it’s hard to just be normal about it. But I don’t know that it’s like I physically can’t stop.\n\nAt work it’s different too, because you’ve only got so much time, and someone’s always banging a pallet jack into something or the microwave is beeping forever, and half the time I’m more focused on getting back than on whether I’m full or not. So… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it as “I can’t stop once I start.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating a normal amount of food, but then you continue eating even after you feel full?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “full” is kind of a weird word for me, because sometimes you eat and you’re supposed to feel something, but mostly I’m just thinking about whether it was too much or whether I should’ve had something different.\n\nLike at work, if I bring something, I’m usually watching the clock more than anything. The break room is so loud—people scraping chairs, somebody talking about overtime, someone complaining about the vending machine taking their dollar—and I’ll just kind of get through whatever I decided to eat. I don’t know if I’d say I keep going after I’m full.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it that way. If anything, I’m usually trying to figure out if I should be eating at all, not thinking, “Oh, I’m full but I’m still going.” But maybe I’m not answering it right.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would be extremely upset if you gained a small amount of weight, like a few pounds?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, a few pounds sounds small when you say it like that, but if I saw it on the scale, I’d probably… I’d panic. Like my head goes straight to, “Okay, this is it, you’re losing control,” and then I start thinking it’s just going to keep going and I’ll end up heavy again.

It’s stupid because at work we measure metal parts down to tiny little differences, and everyone acts like a little variation is normal, machines run hot, whatever—but with me it doesn’t feel like variation. It feels like proof I messed up. So yeah, even a little bit would really upset me.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… occupational health sent me over. They said my weight is, like, dangerously low for what I do at work—running presses and fabrication all day. I work around metal and plastics, and it gets hot, and everyone’s always acting like I’m about to fall over, but I don’t really see it that way.\n\nI just thought I was getting leaner, you know? More disciplined. Like when you get your routine right and things start tightening up. My supervisor made a whole thing of it, though, and then the occupational health nurse got involved, so… that’s why I’m here, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying it like that, because it sounds dramatic. But if the scale goes up even a little, I get this panic, like everything I’ve worked to keep under control is going to just unravel and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nIt’s stupid because it’s just a number, right? But it doesn’t feel like just a number. It feels like… like when a machine starts making the wrong noise and you know something’s about to go off, even if everyone else says it’s fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight?\n\nPatient: Not really… I know people say I do, but when I catch myself in the shop window, or in the bathroom mirror, I don’t see that. I just see my stomach and my thighs, like they’re too big, and then I can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nIt sounds vain, I guess. Like, I’ll be trying to get back to work and I’m still stuck on how I looked for the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… what counts as “large”? Like, people at work will call a sandwich and chips a big lunch, but those guys are standing around the break room eating like it’s nothing, talking about football pools and whose turn it is to clean the press filters.\n\nFor me, I don’t really think of it that way. I can’t tell if something is a large amount or if it just feels large because I’m paying too much attention to it. I guess I’d need to know what you mean by “large,” because my sense of it is kind of messed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “lost control” sounds like… like I’m suddenly eating everything in the cupboards, and I don’t think of it like that.\n\nSometimes I feel kind of foggy around food, like I’m not doing it “right,” but then I get stuck thinking about the exact thing I ate, or whether I should’ve had it earlier in the day. At work, if I eat on break, the machines are so loud and everyone’s coming in and out, and I’ll be trying to act normal, but my head is somewhere else.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that’s losing control, exactly. It’s more like I’m tense the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I don’t like feeling food in my stomach, if that makes sense, but making myself vomit sounds very specific.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it that way. Sometimes after eating I’ll go to the bathroom just to be away from everyone, especially at work because the break room is horrible and there’s always someone banging a locker or asking if I’ve seen some tool they lost. But… I don’t know. I’m not sure what counts as “making yourself,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help with weight loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure. I know what laxatives are, I think, but diuretics—are those the water pills? My aunt used to talk about those because her ankles would swell up in the summer, and she’d sit with her feet up and complain about the humidity.\n\nI mean, I don’t have some routine with that kind of thing, if that’s what you’re asking. I get confused because people talk about “bloating” all the time, especially at work—someone’s always saying they feel puffy after lunch or whatever—and then I start wondering if I’m supposed to be doing something about it, but… no, I don’t think I can give you a clear yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… but I guess I don’t always know what counts as “excessive.” Like, if someone else saw it, they might have a totally different opinion, you know?\n\nAt work, people eat so fast on break because the buzzer goes and then everyone’s back to the line, and someone will have a sandwich and chips and one of those giant gas station drinks, and it just seems normal there. But then I’ll think about what I had and it feels like too much even if it probably wasn’t.\n\nSo… in a short period of time? I haven’t noticed anything like that, not really. I’d probably need you to tell me what you mean by excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific weight or body shape you're trying to achieve?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s a number, exactly. People always talk about numbers like that makes it simple, but it doesn’t feel simple to me.\n\nIt’s more like… when I catch myself in the shop window at work, or in the bathroom mirror, I just zero in on my stomach and my thighs. Like that’s all I can see. They look too big to me, and then it just… it can wreck my whole mood for the rest of the day.\n\nAnd I know the shop windows are warped sometimes—one of the guys was joking about that once because the reflection makes the forklifts look bent—but even knowing that doesn’t really help. I still see it and feel awful.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you're focused on food?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m eating fast,” if that makes sense. Sometimes at work everybody eats quickly because the breaks are so short, and you kind of learn to chew and swallow and get back before someone starts yelling about the line.\n\nBut during times I’m focused on food… I’m not sure I’d call it “much more quickly than usual.” I notice more what it is, or how it feels after, not so much the speed. I might need you to explain what you mean by “during these times.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating an amount of food that would be considered unusual or abnormal by others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “unusual” is hard because everyone I’m around eats different, you know? Like at the plant, some people barely eat on break and some people bring this huge lunch bag like they’re feeding three people, and nobody really says anything unless someone’s food smells strong in the break room.\n\nFor me… I don’t think I’ve had something where other people would be like, “That’s abnormal.” But then I also don’t really like eating where people can see, so maybe I wouldn’t know. Sometimes I’ll look at what’s on my plate and it feels like a lot to me, but I don’t know if someone else would think that or if it’s just me making it into a thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating without being able to stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Like… “can’t stop” sounds really dramatic, like someone in a movie eating straight from the fridge at midnight or something.\n\nI mean, once I start eating, sometimes I get kind of irritated because then I’m thinking about it more, and I’m aware of every bite, and it’s hard to just be normal about it. But I don’t know that it’s like I physically can’t stop.\n\nAt work it’s different too, because you’ve only got so much time, and someone’s always banging a pallet jack into something or the microwave is beeping forever, and half the time I’m more focused on getting back than on whether I’m full or not. So… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it as “I can’t stop once I start.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating a normal amount of food, but then you continue eating even after you feel full?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “full” is kind of a weird word for me, because sometimes you eat and you’re supposed to feel something, but mostly I’m just thinking about whether it was too much or whether I should’ve had something different.\n\nLike at work, if I bring something, I’m usually watching the clock more than anything. The break room is so loud—people scraping chairs, somebody talking about overtime, someone complaining about the vending machine taking their dollar—and I’ll just kind of get through whatever I decided to eat. I don’t know if I’d say I keep going after I’m full.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it that way. If anything, I’m usually trying to figure out if I should be eating at all, not thinking, “Oh, I’m full but I’m still going.” But maybe I’m not answering it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be extremely upset if you gained a small amount of weight, like a few pounds?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, a few pounds sounds small when you say it like that, but if I saw it on the scale, I’d probably… I’d panic. Like my head goes straight to, “Okay, this is it, you’re losing control,” and then I start thinking it’s just going to keep going and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nIt’s stupid because at work we measure metal parts down to tiny little differences, and everyone acts like a little variation is normal, machines run hot, whatever—but with me it doesn’t feel like variation. It feels like proof I messed up. So yeah, even a little bit would really upset me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Sometimes at work everybody eats quickly because the breaks are so short, and you kind of learn to chew and swallow and get back before someone starts yelling about the line.\n\nBut during times I’m focused on food… I’m not sure I’d call it “much more quickly than usual.” I notice more what it is, or how it feels after, not so much the speed. I might need you to explain what you mean by “during these times.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating an amount of food that would be considered unusual or abnormal by others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “unusual” is hard because everyone I’m around eats different, you know? Like at the plant, some people barely eat on break and some people bring this huge lunch bag like they’re feeding three people, and nobody really says anything unless someone’s food smells strong in the break room.\n\nFor me… I don’t think I’ve had something where other people would be like, “That’s abnormal.” But then I also don’t really like eating where people can see, so maybe I wouldn’t know. Sometimes I’ll look at what’s on my plate and it feels like a lot to me, but I don’t know if someone else would think that or if it’s just me making it into a thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating without being able to stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Like… “can’t stop” sounds really dramatic, like someone in a movie eating straight from the fridge at midnight or something.\n\nI mean, once I start eating, sometimes I get kind of irritated because then I’m thinking about it more, and I’m aware of every bite, and it’s hard to just be normal about it. But I don’t know that it’s like I physically can’t stop.\n\nAt work it’s different too, because you’ve only got so much time, and someone’s always banging a pallet jack into something or the microwave is beeping forever, and half the time I’m more focused on getting back than on whether I’m full or not. So… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it as “I can’t stop once I start.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating a normal amount of food, but then you continue eating even after you feel full?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “full” is kind of a weird word for me, because sometimes you eat and you’re supposed to feel something, but mostly I’m just thinking about whether it was too much or whether I should’ve had something different.\n\nLike at work, if I bring something, I’m usually watching the clock more than anything. The break room is so loud—people scraping chairs, somebody talking about overtime, someone complaining about the vending machine taking their dollar—and I’ll just kind of get through whatever I decided to eat. I don’t know if I’d say I keep going after I’m full.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it that way. If anything, I’m usually trying to figure out if I should be eating at all, not thinking, “Oh, I’m full but I’m still going.” But maybe I’m not answering it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be extremely upset if you gained a small amount of weight, like a few pounds?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, a few pounds sounds small when you say it like that, but if I saw it on the scale, I’d probably… I’d panic. Like my head goes straight to, “Okay, this is it, you’re losing control,” and then I start thinking it’s just going to keep going and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nIt’s stupid because at work we measure metal parts down to tiny little differences, and everyone acts like a little variation is normal, machines run hot, whatever—but with me it doesn’t feel like variation. It feels like proof I messed up. So yeah, even a little bit would really upset me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I think so. I mean, people will say I look “fine” or whatever, but when I catch myself in the shop window, like walking past the glass, I don’t see fine. I just zero in on my stomach, or my thighs, and then that’s it. It’s like the whole day is ruined.

I work around metal and plastics, and it gets hot, and everyone’s always acting like I’m about to fall over, but I don’t really see it that way.\n\nI just thought I was getting leaner, you know? More disciplined. Like when you get your routine right and things start tightening up. My supervisor made a whole thing of it, though, and then the occupational health nurse got involved, so… that’s why I’m here, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying it like that, because it sounds dramatic. But if the scale goes up even a little, I get this panic, like everything I’ve worked to keep under control is going to just unravel and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nIt’s stupid because it’s just a number, right? But it doesn’t feel like just a number. It feels like… like when a machine starts making the wrong noise and you know something’s about to go off, even if everyone else says it’s fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight?\n\nPatient: Not really… I know people say I do, but when I catch myself in the shop window, or in the bathroom mirror, I don’t see that. I just see my stomach and my thighs, like they’re too big, and then I can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nIt sounds vain, I guess. Like, I’ll be trying to get back to work and I’m still stuck on how I looked for the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… what counts as “large”? Like, people at work will call a sandwich and chips a big lunch, but those guys are standing around the break room eating like it’s nothing, talking about football pools and whose turn it is to clean the press filters.\n\nFor me, I don’t really think of it that way. I can’t tell if something is a large amount or if it just feels large because I’m paying too much attention to it. I guess I’d need to know what you mean by “large,” because my sense of it is kind of messed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “lost control” sounds like… like I’m suddenly eating everything in the cupboards, and I don’t think of it like that.\n\nSometimes I feel kind of foggy around food, like I’m not doing it “right,” but then I get stuck thinking about the exact thing I ate, or whether I should’ve had it earlier in the day. At work, if I eat on break, the machines are so loud and everyone’s coming in and out, and I’ll be trying to act normal, but my head is somewhere else.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that’s losing control, exactly. It’s more like I’m tense the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I don’t like feeling food in my stomach, if that makes sense, but making myself vomit sounds very specific.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it that way. Sometimes after eating I’ll go to the bathroom just to be away from everyone, especially at work because the break room is horrible and there’s always someone banging a locker or asking if I’ve seen some tool they lost. But… I don’t know. I’m not sure what counts as “making yourself,” exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to help with weight loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure. I know what laxatives are, I think, but diuretics—are those the water pills? My aunt used to talk about those because her ankles would swell up in the summer, and she’d sit with her feet up and complain about the humidity.\n\nI mean, I don’t have some routine with that kind of thing, if that’s what you’re asking. I get confused because people talk about “bloating” all the time, especially at work—someone’s always saying they feel puffy after lunch or whatever—and then I start wondering if I’m supposed to be doing something about it, but… no, I don’t think I can give you a clear yes to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… but I guess I don’t always know what counts as “excessive.” Like, if someone else saw it, they might have a totally different opinion, you know?\n\nAt work, people eat so fast on break because the buzzer goes and then everyone’s back to the line, and someone will have a sandwich and chips and one of those giant gas station drinks, and it just seems normal there. But then I’ll think about what I had and it feels like too much even if it probably wasn’t.\n\nSo… in a short period of time? I haven’t noticed anything like that, not really. I’d probably need you to tell me what you mean by excessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a specific weight or body shape you're trying to achieve?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s a number, exactly. People always talk about numbers like that makes it simple, but it doesn’t feel simple to me.\n\nIt’s more like… when I catch myself in the shop window at work, or in the bathroom mirror, I just zero in on my stomach and my thighs. Like that’s all I can see. They look too big to me, and then it just… it can wreck my whole mood for the rest of the day.\n\nAnd I know the shop windows are warped sometimes—one of the guys was joking about that once because the reflection makes the forklifts look bent—but even knowing that doesn’t really help. I still see it and feel awful.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you're focused on food?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m eating fast,” if that makes sense. Sometimes at work everybody eats quickly because the breaks are so short, and you kind of learn to chew and swallow and get back before someone starts yelling about the line.\n\nBut during times I’m focused on food… I’m not sure I’d call it “much more quickly than usual.” I notice more what it is, or how it feels after, not so much the speed. I might need you to explain what you mean by “during these times.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating an amount of food that would be considered unusual or abnormal by others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “unusual” is hard because everyone I’m around eats different, you know? Like at the plant, some people barely eat on break and some people bring this huge lunch bag like they’re feeding three people, and nobody really says anything unless someone’s food smells strong in the break room.\n\nFor me… I don’t think I’ve had something where other people would be like, “That’s abnormal.” But then I also don’t really like eating where people can see, so maybe I wouldn’t know. Sometimes I’ll look at what’s on my plate and it feels like a lot to me, but I don’t know if someone else would think that or if it’s just me making it into a thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating without being able to stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way. Like… “can’t stop” sounds really dramatic, like someone in a movie eating straight from the fridge at midnight or something.\n\nI mean, once I start eating, sometimes I get kind of irritated because then I’m thinking about it more, and I’m aware of every bite, and it’s hard to just be normal about it. But I don’t know that it’s like I physically can’t stop.\n\nAt work it’s different too, because you’ve only got so much time, and someone’s always banging a pallet jack into something or the microwave is beeping forever, and half the time I’m more focused on getting back than on whether I’m full or not. So… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it as “I can’t stop once I start.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating a normal amount of food, but then you continue eating even after you feel full?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, “full” is kind of a weird word for me, because sometimes you eat and you’re supposed to feel something, but mostly I’m just thinking about whether it was too much or whether I should’ve had something different.\n\nLike at work, if I bring something, I’m usually watching the clock more than anything. The break room is so loud—people scraping chairs, somebody talking about overtime, someone complaining about the vending machine taking their dollar—and I’ll just kind of get through whatever I decided to eat. I don’t know if I’d say I keep going after I’m full.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it that way. If anything, I’m usually trying to figure out if I should be eating at all, not thinking, “Oh, I’m full but I’m still going.” But maybe I’m not answering it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be extremely upset if you gained a small amount of weight, like a few pounds?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, a few pounds sounds small when you say it like that, but if I saw it on the scale, I’d probably… I’d panic. Like my head goes straight to, “Okay, this is it, you’re losing control,” and then I start thinking it’s just going to keep going and I’ll end up heavy again.\n\nIt’s stupid because at work we measure metal parts down to tiny little differences, and everyone acts like a little variation is normal, machines run hot, whatever—but with me it doesn’t feel like variation. It feels like proof I messed up. So yeah, even a little bit would really upset me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, people will say I look “fine” or whatever, but when I catch myself in the shop window, like walking past the glass, I don’t see fine. I just zero in on my stomach, or my thighs, and then that’s it. It’s like the whole day is ruined.\n\nAnd the bathroom mirror at work is awful too, the lighting is that weird gray-yellow kind, and there’s always someone knocking or the sink dripping, and I’ll stand there thinking my legs look huge or my middle looks… just wrong. Even if I was having an okay morning, seeing that can change my whole mood. So yeah, I guess what I see must be different from what other people say they see.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yeah, it would. Even a pound or two… I know that sounds ridiculous, like it’s barely anything, but if I saw the number go up, my whole day would kind of tilt.

I’d start thinking, “You’re losing it, you’re losing control,” and then I can’t just leave it at that. My mind jumps ahead, like it’s going to keep going and I’ll end up heavy again. And then I’m distracted at work, messing up simple measurements or checking things twice, and everyone’s talking about weekend plans or the press being down again, and I’m just stuck on that number.

So… yes. It would affect my mood a lot. More than I want to admit.
